# NEEC Report v2.0, Part I: the scorecards, domain by domain

> **Draft (Session 84, ledger S41).** Johnson and Claude (Anthropic), 2026. The scores are those of the `s84` state:
> the rescoring pass on the v2.0 criteria (Sessions 37 to 65), the blind re-read (Sessions 71 to 77), the owner's decisions
> (58.1, 79.1 to 79.4, Session 79; D66 and D67, Session 84), the adjudication of the outside blind review (Session 83) and
> Session 84's two consistency readings. They change only through the push-back route, by a recorded pass applied by a
> generator. Every scorecard below is generated by `build_report_v2.py` from the units' own readings (decision 52.8); the
> prose between the scorecards is written by hand and reports what the scorecards show. The full sources for every unit
> are in the evidence reports (`evidence/<code>.md`), linked from each entry.

## How to read Part I

Each of the 29 criteria has one scorecard. It gives the v2.0 Pass Threshold, how many of the 23 entries score 1.0, 0.5 and
0.0 now and did in v1 (on the 26 criteria v1 scored), and then each entry in the order of the standing: its v2.0 score, its
v1 score, and the reading behind it. A cell that a unit of v2.0 read shows one line per clause of the threshold, with its
status (cleared, short, not shown, out of reach, or moot) and the reading; a cell no unit read shows the published v1
rationale, quoted. A score of 1.0 needs every clause cleared on an estimate; any clause not cleared makes it 0.5; 0.0 is a
structural failure, which needs one of protocol 2.1's conditions for the criterion as a whole, stated beside it. A close call
is a score a reasonable scorer could set differently; it names the other score and the reading that supports it.

**A note on what changed between v1 and v2.0.** v2.0 restated or sharpened most thresholds (each scorecard names its
revision class), and the pass read each cell clause by clause against them. Most of the movement is downward, and most of
it has one cause: a v1 score of 1.0 that rested on a design's description of what it would achieve, with no estimate of
the quantity the threshold names. Under v2.0's rule such a clause is not shown, and the cell is 0.5. A fall of this kind
records what the sources establish, not a finding that the system performs worse.

**The entry's name.** From Session 84 the owner's design is shown as CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP (ledger D68). NEEC's records
before Session 84, and quotations from them, call it CCO-PTF-CIP-SZH; it is the same entry, read on the same sources.

**Disclosure.** CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP was designed by Duke Johnson, one of NEEC's two authors. Its first place rests on the
owner's decisions; on the readings alone it would stand fifth (the standing below; `NEEC_OwnerItems_s84.md`, section 5).
The Paper states this in its body.

## Domain 1: Material Security

Six criteria: poverty (C1.1), wealth building (C1.2a) and its prevention of concentration (C1.2b), housing (C1.3),
automation (C1.4) and access to a wealth pathway (C1.5). v1 scored 32 of these 138 cells at 1.0; v2.0 scores three, and no
entry clears more than one of the six. The fall is the domain's main finding, and it is a finding about evidence: the
thresholds name quantities (a reduction in the poverty headcount over 20 years, a wealth Gini, a housing stability rate,
poverty and demand under 30%, 50% and 70% displacement of paid hours), and almost no source, for a design or for an
economy, states them.

**C1.1, poverty.** None of the nine v1 passes holds; 21 entries score 0.5 and two 0.0. The threshold asks for a 90%
reduction within 20 years and 85% under stress. Seven designs clear the first clause on their own projections (Market
Socialism, Participatory Economics, Universal Basic Income, Degrowth, MMT + Job Guarantee, Fully Automated Luxury Communism,
and Centrally Planned Socialism on its record of absolute poverty), but none states a figure under stress; where a stress
episode is documented, as Cuba's Special Period is for Centrally Planned Socialism, it records a fall in food intake, not a
held reduction. The configured economies
are read on v2.0's indicator, the World Bank's Societal Poverty Line, and on it the headcount rose: in the United States
from 19.2% (2005) to 20.2% (2024), and in each Nordic economy from 2005 to 2023 (Sweden 9.8% to 12.4%), which is why Nordic
Social Democracy falls from 1.0. CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP is read on its own model (v5.6.0, 500 seeds): poverty on the line is
7.7% at year 20 against 7.9% with no programme, and higher than with no programme in the adverse environment. The two
failures are Libertarian Minarchism, whose sources leave poverty relief to private charity, and Doughnut Economics, which
names the target and adopts no instrument to meet it (a fall from 0.5; a close call toward 0.5).

**C1.2a, wealth building.** One entry passes: CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP, on the owner's decision 79.1 (its close call toward 0.5
records the reading it replaced: the clause holds only if new output backs what the design pays, which its sources do not
state). The three other v1 passes fall: Nordic Social Democracy, because on Norway's register data the 30th percentile of
household net wealth is below the bar (the one Nordic register that publishes the distribution); Singapore, because a median
above the bar does not show 70% of participants; Market Socialism, for want of any figure. MMT + Job Guarantee falls to 0.0
as a correction (a wage holds no asset, the 0.0 band, as for Universal Basic Income and Georgism's dividend), and Libertarian
Minarchism rises to 0.5 (a shortfall of reach is 0.5 under protocol 2.1, as for Status Quo).

**C1.2b, preventing concentration.** No entry passes; v1 had five passes. Four were class W cells the pass never re-read,
whose 1.0 rested on the 1.0 band's description of a structural mechanism with no estimate of the wealth distribution; the
adjudication of the outside review lowered Degrowth's, Integral's and Mutual Credit's, and Session 84 Participatory
Economics' by the same rule (one reading per class): a bound on the top of the distribution does not bound a Gini, which
the bottom also sets. CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP is read on its model, which gives a wealth Gini of 0.49 at year 20 against 0.76
with no programme: a large reduction, short of 0.35. Fully Automated Luxury Communism rises from 0.0 to 0.5. Seventeen cells
are held at their published scores.

**C1.3, housing.** One pass: Centrally Planned Socialism, the band's own anchor (no mortgages, rents unchanged since 1928,
housing fees near 3% of income at 80% of the median), with a close call toward 0.5. Eight v1 passes fall. The designs state
no stability rate and no housing cost at 80% of the median; CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP's model has no housing tenure or rent line to
compute them. Nordic Social Democracy is stable but short on cost (in Denmark's lowest income quintile 44.6% to 60.4% of
households spend more than 40% of disposable income on housing, across the EU-SILC series), and Singapore is stable for
residents but not for the rental segment, which houses most non-residents.

**C1.4, automation.** No entry passes; v1 had four passes. No source, for any entry, models poverty and aggregate demand in
the three displacement scenarios, and CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP's model, which v2.0 names, cannot yet run them. A mechanism that
decouples income from work (Universal Basic Income, Participatory Economics' average income for those who cannot work) is
credited at 0.5, not 1.0. Five entries fail, each on protocol 2.1's first condition (no mechanism addresses the criterion):
Islamic Finance, Status Quo, Stakeholder Capitalism, Libertarian Minarchism and Doughnut Economics, under each of which
income and demand run through paid work with nothing added for displacement.

**C1.5, access to a wealth pathway.** A class W criterion, so the pass held 22 cells at their published scores; Nordic
Social Democracy keeps the domain's other 1.0. The one cell re-read, Libertarian Minarchism's, rose from 0.0 to 0.5 in the
adjudication (markets open to anyone, gated by income rather than prior wealth; no figure either way), with a close call
toward 0.0.

**What the domain does not show.** Its 39 held cells, all on the two class W criteria (C1.2b and C1.5), rest on v1
rationales the pass did not re-read, because v2.0 changed only their wording; the outside blind reader agreed with 27 of
them, and the adjudication held the other 12 on their sources. The limits page states which held cells no reading of v2.0 reached.

<!-- generated:D1 -->
### C1.1 Poverty Elimination Capacity

Pass Threshold (v2.0): ≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario, ≥85% under stress testing. Revision class I (indicator named for an unchanged clause and bar).

v2.0: 0 at 1.0, 21 at 0.5, 2 at 0.0. v1: 9 at 1.0, 13 at 0.5, 1 at 0.0.

**CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/cco.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: short. on the design's published model at v5.6.0 (500 seeds, 20 years, the Reference environment), poverty on the Societal Poverty Line, v2.0's indicator, computed from the engine's disposable income per adult, is 7.7% at year 20 in the release row (nothing the Source pays backed by new output, the model's main reading) against 7.9% in the paired run with no programme and today's safety net, a reduction of 1.5%, and 9.5% at full backing; with the in-kind value of price cuts counted, 4.6%; it was 7.3% at adoption, so it rises over the run; the largest reduction on any measure the engine reports is 74.5% (below 30 days of basic living, at full backing), and the release's own measure, adults below their cost of living, falls 60.3% (release row) and 70.7% (full backing) against no programme (readings 3.3, 3.4)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: short. in the Adverse environment (recessions, an automation wave and 2% outside inflation), poverty on the Societal Poverty Line is 8.3% at year 20 in the release row and 9.7% at full backing, against 7.3% with no programme, higher in both; the share below the cost of living falls 52.9% against no programme; no measure falls 85% (reading 3.4)

Read in group 2.5 (Session 57).

**Nordic Social Democracy: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/nsd.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: short. short: the headcount at the Societal Poverty Line rose in each of the four economies over the window -- DNK 6.6% in 2005 to 7.4% in 2023, a reduction of -12.1%; FIN 7.3% in 2005 to 8.7% in 2023, a reduction of -19.2%; NOR 7.2% in 2005 to 8.5% in 2023, a reduction of -18.1%; SWE 9.8% in 2005 to 12.4% in 2023, a reduction of -26.5%; the lines rose with the medians (Sweden's from $21.92 to $29.10 a day), and the rates, among the world's lowest, did not fall (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: short. short: below 85% in every economy; the rates rose after 2008 (Sweden 10.1% to 10.8% by 2012) (reading 1.1.2)

Read in part (b3) (Session 40) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**Market Socialism: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/ms.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: cleared. demonstrate poverty elimination capacity
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. the documented stress episode is Spain's 2008-13 crisis: Fagor Electrodomesticos, the group's founding cooperative, failed in 2013 with about 5,700 employees; the group's mechanisms (relocation, early retirement, Lagun-Aro) covered its 1,898 members, of whom 417 were relocated within two months with solutions aimed at 1,000-1,200, while non-member employees of its subsidiaries were outside them; no poverty-reduction figure under stress is located for members or for the wider workforce (reading 3.1)

Read in part (b3) (Session 40).

**Participatory Economics: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/pe.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: cleared. would eliminate poverty
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. remuneration by effort and sacrifice and universal access to consumption councils are specified; no stress scenario or estimate is stated or cited (reading 3.1)

Read in part (b3) (Session 40).

**Integral: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/int.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: not shown. not shown: the white paper states that access to essentials is 'structurally guaranteed' and that fair-access protocols keep essentials available 'regardless of individual capacity, disability, or temporary hardship', through the ITC's fairness signal, but sets no level and projects no rate; its access is in non-transferable time credits, which the Societal Poverty Line's income or consumption measure would read only through a projection the paper does not make (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. not shown: no stress scenario for access to essentials (reading 1.1.2)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**Sovereign Wealth Fund Statism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/swf.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: short. short: Alaska's dividend (about $1,000 to $3,300 a person a year) is a tenth to a quarter of a line of $13,627 a year; Norway's variant funds the budget, and Norway's headcount at the line rose from 7.2% in 2005 to 8.5% in 2023 with the fund at four times mainland GDP (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: short. short: below 85% on both variants (reading 1.1.2)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**Ostrom-Style Commons Governance: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/os.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: out of reach. out of reach: the mechanism governs the appropriation and provision rules of common-pool resources, not household income or consumption at large; generalised to every commons, wage and transfer incomes stay outside it (reading 1.1.3)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: out of reach. out of reach, as clause 1 (reading 1.1.3)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**Universal Basic Income: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/ubi.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: cleared. would achieve 95%+ elimination
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. no estimate under stress is stated or cited; the payment is specified as unconditional and fixed, part (b2) found no rule raising it with crisis severity, and Alaska's dividend, the long-running precedent, is set on an annual cycle (reading 3.1)

Read in part (b3) (Session 40).

**Degrowth Economics: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/dg.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: cleared. could eliminate poverty
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. the rationale names instruments (wealth caps, maximum income ratios, universal basic services) that could eliminate poverty; no stress scenario or estimate is stated or cited (reading 3.1)

Read in part (b3) (Session 40).

**Georgism / Land Value Tax (LVT + Citizen's Dividend): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/geo.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: short. short: the citizen's dividend governs part of household income; distributing the whole of US land rent (on the order of 5 to 10% of GDP, Larson 2015) would pay roughly $3,000 to $6,000 a person a year, against a line of $37.33 a day ($13,627 a year) that itself rises by half the median's gain; a floor that low lifts part of the poor over the line, far from nine in ten (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: short. short: land rent falls in a downturn, and the dividend with it (reading 1.1.2)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**Islamic Finance / Profit-Sharing Banking: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/if.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: out of reach. out of reach: the profit-and-loss-sharing contract set allocates finance; zakat and the social-finance layer lie outside it (the extension test, D31: the Islamic finance precedent), so the mechanism governs no floor under household income (reading 1.1.3)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: out of reach. out of reach, as clause 1 (reading 1.1.3)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**Mutual Credit / LETS: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/mc.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: not shown. not shown: mutual credit lets members with skills but no money trade through interest-free credit lines; the schemes' studies report gains to participants (the UK's LETS, the Bangla-Pesa) and no headcount at a national line, and a credit line transfers no income to those who cannot supply (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. not shown: no stress scenario (reading 1.1.2)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**State Capitalism / Singapore (GLC Developmental Capitalism): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/sg.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: not shown. not shown: the Poverty and Inequality Platform holds no Singapore survey and the state publishes no poverty line; the one academic estimate (Donaldson et al., 2013) is a single basic-needs level, not a change over the window (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. not shown: no series, so no stress reading (reading 1.1.2)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**Status Quo Market Capitalism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/sq.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: short. short: the United States' headcount at the Societal Poverty Line went from 19.2% in 2005 to 20.2% in 2024, a reduction of -5.2% -- no reduction; the line rose with the median ($30.84 to $37.33 a day, 2021 PPP) and the headcount stayed near a fifth throughout (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: short. short: the base reduction is below 85%; the 2020-21 transfers cut the rate to 16.7% in 2021, and it returned to 19.5% in 2022 when they lapsed (reading 1.1.2)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.0. 0.5: transfer programmes are a real mechanism (they cut the rate in 2021), but the headcount did not fall

**Universal Basic Services: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/ubs.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: not shown. not shown: the services are in kind, and the Societal Poverty Line counts income or consumption; the proposal's sources value the services to a low-income household (the Institute for Global Prosperity, 2017) but project no headcount at any line (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. not shown: no stress scenario (reading 1.1.2)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**MMT + Job Guarantee: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/mmt.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: cleared. Near-universal poverty elimination achievable
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. no estimate of poverty reduction under stress is stated or cited; the guarantee is specified as countercyclical, which is a mechanism, not an estimate of the level (reading 3.1); the one crisis-tested precedent of the component, Argentina's Plan Jefes y Jefas in the 2002 crisis, in which poverty rose from 37% to 58%, cut the share of participants falling into indigence from an estimated 40% to 30%

Read in part (b3) (Session 40).

**Fully Automated Luxury Communism: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/falc.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: cleared. Theoretical capacity for 100% poverty elimination
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. the rationale conditions its figure on technological assumptions proving correct; no scenario in which they fall short, this entry's stress case, is estimated (reading 3.1)

Read in part (b3) (Session 40).

**Stakeholder Capitalism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/sc.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: short. short: the reform changes corporate purpose and governance (living wages and fair taxes as aims) and leaves the host's transfers unmodified; on its principal host's record the headcount went from 19.2% in 2005 to 20.2% in 2024, a reduction of -5.2% (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: short. short: below 85% on the host's record (reading 1.1.2)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**State Capitalism / China (Party-State-Directed Market Economy): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/cn.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: short. short: China's headcount at the Societal Poverty Line (consumption) went from 39.1% in 2005 to 19.7% in 2022, a reduction of 49.6%, a real reduction about half the bar's; the line itself rose from $3.38 to $8.02 a day as the median grew (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: short. short: the base reduction is below 85%; the rate kept falling through 2020 (21.0%) and rose in 2022 (19.2% to 19.7%) (reading 1.1.2)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**State Capitalism / Qatar (Gulf Rentier-Distributive Statism): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/qa.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: not shown. not shown: the Platform holds one Qatar survey (17.6% at the line in 2017), so no reduction can be computed (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. not shown: no series (reading 1.1.2)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.0. 0.5: the rentier state's transfers are a real mechanism for citizens; the migrant majority is outside them

**Libertarian Minarchism: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/lm.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: short. short: the platform phases out government income security and holds that 'the proper and most effective source of help for the poor is the voluntary efforts of private groups and individuals'; no public floor exists to project (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: short. short: private charity, the only mechanism, is itself procyclical under stress (reading 1.1.2)

Why 0.0 stands: protocol 2.1, second condition: the system's own sources refuse poverty relief as a public function and leave it to private charity (reading 1.1.4)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

**Doughnut Economics: 0.0** (v1 0.5, down). [Evidence](evidence/de.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: not shown. not shown: the social foundation's income dimension (no one below the poverty line) is a target, and 'Design to Distribute' offers land, money creation, enterprise, knowledge and basic income as options, not as an adopted design; no rate is projected (reading 1.1.1)
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: not shown. not shown: no stress scenario (reading 1.1.2)

Why 0.0 stands: protocol 2.1, first condition: no structural mechanism addresses poverty -- the framework names the target and adopts no instrument to meet it (reading 1.1.4)

Read in group 2.11 (Session 65) and re-read blind in Session 74.

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.5. 0.0: a target without an adopted mechanism

**Centrally Planned Socialism: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/cps.md#c1-1)

1. *≥90% poverty reduction within 20 years under base scenario*: cleared. near-universal elimination of absolute poverty
2. *≥85% under stress testing*: short. Cuba's Special Period (1990-95), after the loss of Soviet support, is a documented stress episode in a centrally planned economy with universal rationing: per capita daily energy intake fell from 2,899 to 1,863 kcal and average adult weight by 4-5 kg, a neuropathy outbreak, possibly due to vitamin deficiencies, affected about 50,000 people in 1992-93, and the decline in infant mortality reversed in 1990-93; a special rationing system shielded children, elderly people and pregnant women from the outbreak, but a basic necessity, food, fell short for the adult population at large

Read in part (b3) (Session 40).

### C1.2a Wealth Building for Resilience

Pass Threshold (v2.0): ≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity. Revision class M (bar, measure or clause restated).

v2.0: 1 at 1.0, 12 at 0.5, 10 at 0.0. v1: 4 at 1.0, 9 at 0.5, 10 at 0.0.

**CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP: 1.0** (v1 1.0). [Evidence](evidence/cco.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: cleared. on the owner's reading (decision 79.1, as decision 58.1 reads C3.2), the backing of the currency by creative output, a community chest, is an original feature of the design and is read as backing what the Source pays; at full backing the design's published model at v5.6.0 (500 seeds, Reference) has 91.1% of participants reach net wealth with Acre Equity of $60,000 in 2019 dollars at year 20, in 500 of 500 seeds, median $437,409 (77.9% in the Adverse environment), and its BLEI ladder puts 76.2% of adults at Flourishing (two years of basic living and more); in the main reading, with no backing, the figures are 13.9% and 3.4% (48.0% Flourishing with no programme); held until an empirical study shows that the backing does not hold in real-world data

Read in group 2.5 (Session 57), then raised by the owner's decision 79.1 (Session 79).

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.5. Group 2.5's reading (decision 57.3): the clause holds only if new output backs what the Source pays, a condition the design's sources do not state; the release row shows 13.9%

**Nordic Social Democracy: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/nsd.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: short. no figure in the published rationale, which credited the vehicles for those who use them and left breadth to C1.5; on Norway's register data for 2024 the bar is NOK 734,447, and the 30th percentile of household net wealth is NOK 390,300 ($31,885 in 2019 dollars at PPP) while the 40th is NOK 1,131,000 ($92,396): between 60% and 70% of households hold the bar's figure, and 15.8% hold negative net wealth; the register excludes pension wealth, so collective and public pension entitlements are not counted (reading 3.5)

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 1.0. Published 1.0 read in the D28 form: the clause is short on the one Nordic register that publishes the distribution, so the unit falls to 0.5; Sweden publishes no household wealth statistics, and no Nordic source located shows 70% of househo

**Market Socialism: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/ms.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: not shown. the rationale's evidence is qualitative: Mondragón members 'accumulate substantial wealth through capital accounts and profit distribution'; no source the unit cites states an accumulation figure, or the share of participants whose capital accounts and other net worth reach $60,000 (2019 dollars) over 20 years; a genuine, individually held vehicle, not shown at the bar for 70% of participants

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**Participatory Economics: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/pe.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: not shown. no private accumulation; named community investment councils can build collective wealth; no figure or household buffer is stated; the 0.5 band's collectively held case

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**Integral: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/int.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: short. Integral Time Credits cannot be saved or accumulated by design; no other vehicle; the 0.0 band's anchor case

Why 0.0 stands: the system excludes accumulation by design (the band's anchor)

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**Sovereign Wealth Fund Statism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/swf.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: short. a large, growing, professionally invested corpus held collectively; no individual holds a titled or severable claim, so no household's net worth includes it; the 0.5 band's collectively held case

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**Ostrom-Style Commons Governance: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/os.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: short. a member's claim on the commons is a use-right, non-severable and extinguished on exit; no individual balance grows; no case reports household accumulation of the bar's order

Why 0.0 stands: no structural mechanism addresses the criterion: use-rights, not assets (the published flag toward 0.5 stands)

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**Universal Basic Income: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/ubi.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: out of reach. an income transfer; no accumulation vehicle; the 0.0 band's own anchor case

Why 0.0 stands: no structural mechanism addresses the criterion: income only (the band's anchor)

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**Degrowth Economics: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/dg.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: not shown. community land trusts and cooperatives give real, collectively held security; individual accumulation is discouraged by design, and no source states a household figure; the 0.5 band's anchor case

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**Georgism / Land Value Tax (LVT + Citizen's Dividend): 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/geo.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: out of reach. the citizen's dividend is an annual pass-through of land rent, income rather than a held corpus; lower taxes on income and capital raise saving capacity in the host economy, which the reach rule does not credit

Why 0.0 stands: no structural mechanism addresses the criterion: the mechanism's payment is income only

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**Islamic Finance / Profit-Sharing Banking: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/if.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: not shown. profit-sharing investment accounts are a genuine, individually held, accumulating vehicle; returns are smoothed to market benchmarks, so accumulation is indistinguishable from bank saving in the host economy, which the reach rule does not credit; nothing shows 70% of participants at the bar through the mechanism

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**Mutual Credit / LETS: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/mc.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: short. credit balances are claims on other members in a non-convertible unit, capped and in some designs subject to demurrage; no store of value usable as a buffer, for any participant

Why 0.0 stands: no structural mechanism addresses the criterion: the instrument has no accumulation function by design

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**State Capitalism / Singapore (GLC Developmental Capitalism): 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/sg.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: not shown. UBS's median wealth per adult, $96,434 at end-2025, is $100,395 in 2019 dollars at PPP, above the bar; but the clause asks for 70% of participants, and the scoring document estimates that about 75% of the population has a genuine vehicle, Work Permit holders (about a fifth) having none; no published distribution shows 70% of all who live and work in the economy at the figure, and 20-year accumulation, lease decay aside, is not reported

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**Status Quo Market Capitalism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/sq.md#c1-2a)

1. *≥$60,000 median net-wealth accumulation over 20 years for 70%+ of participants, in constant 2019 US dollars at purchasing-power parity*: short. on v2.0's price basis the bar is $75,557 in 2025 dollars; in the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances 66.3% of US families hold net worth at or above it, a lifetime stock that overstates 20 years' accumulation, so fewer than 70% reach the figure; the 30th percentile is $43,835 and the median $171,606 (2019 dollars); among families whose head is 45 to 54, about 20 to 30 years into working life, 71.6% hold the bar's figure, before subtracting what they held at the start (reading 3.5); homeownership and retirement accounts are genuine vehicles, so the unit is 0.5, not 0.0

**Universal Basic Services: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/ubs.md#c1-2a)

Why 0.0 stands: no structural mechanism addresses the criterion: services, not assets

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

**MMT + Job Guarantee: 0.0** (v1 0.5, down). [Evidence](evidence/mmt.md#c1-2a)

Why 0.0 stands: no structural mechanism addresses the criterion: income only, the 0.0 band's own description (Appendix H.7v2)

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

Why 0.0 stands: no structural mechanism addresses the criterion

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

UBS's median wealth per adult, $27,273 at end-2022, is $43,071 in 2019 dollars at PPP, under the bar, so fewer than half of adults hold the figure; v2.0 removes the middle-income adjustment on which the published rationale judged the magnitude likely to clear; homeownership near 90% is a genuine, individually titled vehicle, impaired by falling prices and restricted rural land rights

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

Why 0.0 stands: no structural mechanism addresses the criterion, and the historical record, collapse included (protocol 4.1), shows no credible pathway under the system's own logic

Read in group 2.2 (Session 53).

### C1.2b Prevention of Exploitative Accumulation

Pass Threshold (v2.0): Gini <0.35 for wealth distribution. Revision class W (wording, commentary or disclosure only).

v2.0: 0 at 1.0, 15 at 0.5, 8 at 0.0. v1: 5 at 1.0, 9 at 0.5, 9 at 0.0.

**CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/cco.md#c1-2b)

1. *Gini <0.35 for wealth distribution*: short. on the design's published model at v5.6.0 (500 seeds, Reference), the wealth Gini at year 20, as the engine reports it (debts as zero), is 0.492 in the release row (0.477 with Acre Equity) and 0.602 at full backing, against 0.755 with no programme; no seed is below 0.35 in any run or environment; the design narrows the distribution, but not to the bar (reading 3.6)

Read in group 2.5 (Session 57).

**Nordic Social Democracy: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/nsd.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> Wealth Gini runs 0.65-0.75 — a real, meaningful reduction from unregulated capitalism's 0.85 through genuine redistributive policy — but well above the <0.35 threshold this criterion requires, with no structural cap holding concentration down beyond ordinary progressive taxation.

**Market Socialism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/ms.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> Wealth Gini in cooperative networks runs 0.40-0.50 — real, meaningful progress attributable to distributed ownership over unregulated capitalism's 0.85, but not confirmed under the 0.35 threshold, and membership-based capital accounts do not by themselves cap concentration among cooperative-sector participants.

**Participatory Economics: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/pe.md#c1-2b)

1. *Gini <0.35 for wealth distribution*: not shown. not shown: the design removes private productive property, capital markets and income from ownership, and rates incomes by effort and sacrifice, which bounds the top of the distribution more firmly than Degrowth's caps or Integral's parallel network; but it states no bound on personal holdings, which unequal effort ratings and saving across a lifetime leave unequal, and no source the unit cites, and none the outside reader names (it projects the Gini from the design's mechanisms, closed book), estimates the wealth Gini; a bound on the top does not bound a Gini, which the bottom of the distribution also sets

Read in Session 84, then read by rule 4 of decision 83.4 (decision 84.4, Session 84).

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 1.0. The published reading and the outside reader's: no private capital ownership is a structural ceiling of the kind the 1.0 band describes

**Integral: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/int.md#c1-2b)

1. *Gini <0.35 for wealth distribution*: not shown. not shown: inside the network time-decaying, non-transferable credits and node commons make accumulation impossible, but the design 'is designed to emerge gradually, non-coercively, and in parallel with existing market and state systems', becoming 'supplementary' before 'essential', and no source estimates the wealth held by its population, inside the network or outside it; Jev reads the clause not shown in both passes

Read in the adjudication of the outside review (Session 83).

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 1.0. The published reading: no stock of wealth exists anywhere in the network for any participant to convert into leverage over others

**Sovereign Wealth Fund Statism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/swf.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> The mechanism targets a specific, well-documented channel of wealth concentration — state-owned subsoil resource rent — arguably more completely than Georgism's LVT does for land value: under Norwegian and Alaskan mineral-rights law, this revenue was never privately held to begin with, so there is no equivalent of LVT's ongoing tax-collection contest against an existing private-property interest. But it is narrow in exactly the way Georgism's own C1.2b write-up already flags for its own mechanism: it does nothing about wealth concentration through equity ownership, business profits, or housing, and — a further, distinct point worth naming since it doesn't apply to Georgism — the fund's own growth strategy (roughly 71% global listed equities for GPFG) is not independent of, but deeply reliant on, the same corporate-profit and capital-market dynamics that drive concentration elsewhere in the economy. Norway itself is the clearest illustration of the resulting limits: income inequality there remains among the world's lowest, but wealth concentration at the top is real and, per a March 2026 analysis, rising — just ten people in Norway now own more wealth than the bottom 50% of the population combined, entirely outside anything the fund itself touches, since the fund is a public asset that leaves private equity and business ownership untouched. Alaska's own measured effect on income distribution is small and precisely estimated: a 0.02 reduction in the Gini coefficient (Berman, 2024) — real, but modest. *(continues in the scoring document)*

**Ostrom-Style Commons Governance: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/os.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> **Within the resource, accumulation is structurally blocked.** Boundaries (principle 1), appropriation limits fitted to the resource (principle 2), and graduated sanctions (principle 5) exist precisely to stop any party from taking more than its share. No owner can buy the commons out; that is what makes it a commons. *(continues in the scoring document)*

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 1.0.

**Universal Basic Income: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/ubi.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> **STRUCTURAL FAILURE.** UBI provides income but not economic power — ownership, wealth, and capital remain exactly as concentrated as before, and recipients remain subordinate to capital owners despite the transfer. No mechanism in a generically-funded UBI targets wealth concentration specifically (a UBI funded specifically by a wealth tax would score differently; this evaluation, like the original, scores UBI as generically proposed).

**Degrowth Economics: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/dg.md#c1-2b)

1. *Gini <0.35 for wealth distribution*: not shown. not shown: the literature proposes caps on income and wealth, commons and universal services, which would lower concentration, but the design keeps private property and markets and no source estimates the wealth distribution; a cap on the top does not bound a Gini, which the bottom of the distribution also sets

Read in the adjudication of the outside review (Session 83).

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 1.0. The published reading: decentralisation, wealth caps and cooperative ownership as a named structural ceiling of the kind the 1.0 band describes

**Georgism / Land Value Tax (LVT + Citizen's Dividend): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/geo.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> This is Georgism's most direct point of contact with any NEEC criterion — capturing unearned land-rent accumulation is the mechanism's entire purpose, targeting a well-documented channel of wealth concentration (including the dynamic where public infrastructure investment inflates private land values that titleholders capture without funding the investment). This is structural, not incidental. It is also narrow: it does nothing about wealth concentration through equity, business profits, or financial assets, all larger components of top-end wealth in most advanced economies. No Gini figure specific to a real Georgist implementation exists to cite, since none has run at single-tax intensity; this score rests on the strength of the targeted mechanism rather than a demonstrated outcome.

**Islamic Finance / Profit-Sharing Banking: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/if.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> This is the first of the three criteria Handoff 21 predicted the practice-versus-design gap would bear on, and the prediction holds. The design argument is that risk sharing splits returns between capital and enterprise rather than guaranteeing the capital holder a claim. The practice is that the dominant contracts deliver a predetermined markup to the financier, the liability side pays a smoothed market return, and nothing in the mechanism caps or claws back concentration. Layered on an unmodified market economy, the wealth distribution is the market's. H.7v2's 0.0 band — "no structural prevention mechanism; concentration compounds without limit" — is met, as it is for Status Quo Market Capitalism and Universal Basic Services. *(continues in the scoring document)*

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.5.

**Mutual Credit / LETS: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/mc.md#c1-2b)

1. *Gini <0.35 for wealth distribution*: out of reach. out of reach: a mutual-credit network governs its members' exchange balances (zero-sum, capped, usually interest-free), not the holdings of land, capital and savings that set an economy's wealth Gini, even generalised (protocol 3.2, D28(c))

Read in the adjudication of the outside review (Session 83).

**State Capitalism / Singapore (GLC Developmental Capitalism): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/sg.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> The Ministry of Finance estimates Singapore's wealth Gini at 0.55 — above its income inequality, but below the 0.6–0.7 range it cites for the United Kingdom, Japan, and Germany (MOF Occasional Paper, 2026, as reported by Mothership). That places Singapore below the "social democracy: 0.65–0.75" band Section 12.3 uses. Two structural features limit particular concentration channels: residential land is state-owned and leased rather than sold (C1.2a), and foreign buyers pay a 60% Additional Buyer's Stamp Duty on any home (Global Property Guide). *(continues in the scoring document)*

**Status Quo Market Capitalism: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/sq.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> **STRUCTURAL FAILURE.** Wealth Gini of 0.85 indicates extreme concentration, and no structural mechanism — no cap, no dissolution, no targeted capture of unearned gains — checks it. This is NEEC's own explicit anchor case for a full failure on this criterion (Paper Appendix H.7v2).

**Universal Basic Services: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/ubs.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> This criterion asks whether the system structurally prevents wealth *concentration* — a Gini-coefficient-relevant question, distinct from C1.2a's resilience-buffer question. This is the single most consequential call in this evaluation, and it was revised once during drafting, disclosed here rather than silently settled. **The case for 0.5** (this evaluation's initial read): the shelter sector's plan to double the UK's social housing stock by 1.5 million units removes that many households from the private rental market entirely, a real, costed, committed mechanism — unlike Doughnut Economics, which was scored 0.0 here specifically for citing compatible redistributive mechanisms without adopting any of its own, UBS *does* adopt a specific mechanism of its own, which seemed to warrant credit above Doughnut Economics' clean zero, on the model of Georgism's own 0.5 for land value capture. **Why this evaluation lands at 0.0 instead:** on closer comparison with Georgism's actual mechanism, the analogy does not hold. Georgism's land value tax *captures wealth that would otherwise accumulate to landowners* — it is a device that removes an existing, ongoing accumulation from one party's hands. UBS's shelter sector does the opposite kind of thing: it *builds new housing and gives it away at zero rent*, funded by general taxation. This is real and valuable, but it does not capture, cap, or redistribute any existing stock of wealth; it is an expenditure commitment, not a wealth- concentration-prevention mechanism, and — like every other part of the proposal — it says nothing about the Gini coefficient of the population's existing asset holdings. The proposal's funding mechanism (reducing the Personal Allowance) is a tax-and-transfer adjustment, not a wealth tax, asset cap, or land-value-capture device either. On reflection, this evaluation concludes UBS has no C1.2b mechanism of the kind this criterion asks about, at any scale — a different, and weaker, case than either Georgism's or Mutual Credit/LETS's own targeted mechanisms. *(continues in the scoring document)*

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.5. The housing plank's pressure on land and rental values

**MMT + Job Guarantee: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/mmt.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> **STRUCTURAL FAILURE** — *flagged as this Report's single most consequential contestable score.* No wealth tax, asset cap, or other mechanism targets accumulated capital directly; the job guarantee is an income and employment instrument, not one that touches how fast existing capital holdings grow. This system's own Power Distribution score (C4.4=0.5, below) credits the job-guarantee exit option with reducing capital's *bargaining* power — a real effect, but on a different causal channel than this criterion's own wealth-concentration threshold. A future scorer who reads that bargaining-power reduction as sufficient for this criterion's structural-prevention bar should feel free to revise this to 0.5; doing so is the single change that would move this system back to the Potentially Adequate tier.

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.5. C4.4's reduction of capital power read as sufficient

**Fully Automated Luxury Communism: 0.5** (v1 0.0, up). [Evidence](evidence/falc.md#c1-2b)

1. *Gini <0.35 for wealth distribution*: not shown. not shown: common ownership of automated production, universal services and socialised housing are named in the literature as the design's answer to concentration, but who owns and controls the machines is left unresolved (the entry's own C4.4) and no source estimates the distribution

Read in the adjudication of the outside review (Session 83).

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.0. The published reading: the unresolved control of the machines read as no structural prevention

**Stakeholder Capitalism: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/sc.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> **STRUCTURAL FAILURE.** One-share-one-vote maintains wealth-based power concentration; token stakeholder voice does not challenge fundamental plutocratic control, and no structural cap on accumulation exists.

**State Capitalism / China (Party-State-Directed Market Economy): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/cn.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> UBS estimates China's wealth Gini at 70.9 (end-2022). That is inside the "social democracy: Gini 0.65–0.75" band that Section 12.3 already uses, and well below North America's 82.3 in the same dataset. The trend, however, runs the wrong way. Piketty, Yang & Zucman find that the top 10% income share rose from 27% to 41% between 1978 and 2015 while the bottom 50% share fell from 27% to 15% — a move from near-Nordic toward US levels — and that the top 10% wealth share reached about 67%, close to the US's 72% (as summarized by Stanford SCCEI). *(continues in the scoring document)*

**State Capitalism / Qatar (Gulf Rentier-Distributive Statism): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/qa.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> No official wealth-distribution figure for Qatar was found. Commentary on UBS's figures says Qatar has the second-best wealth Gini among the 25 countries with the highest median wealth (Yahoo Finance and lovemoney); the underlying number was not retrieved. The allocation described under C1.2a is a real deconcentrating channel: it spreads rent-derived land and housing wealth across the citizen body rather than leaving it to market allocation. *(continues in the scoring document)*

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.0. Citizenship divide, no limit at the top

**Libertarian Minarchism: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/lm.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> **STRUCTURAL FAILURE.** Wealth concentration accelerates without redistributive mechanisms — the literal opposite of prevention, and an explicit design feature rather than an oversight.

**Doughnut Economics: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/de.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> This criterion asks whether the system structurally prevents wealth concentration sufficient to enable governance capture — a Gini <0.35 threshold, per Section 12.3. Doughnut Economics has no cap, dissolution mechanism, progressive-taxation commitment, or land-rent capture of its own; as with C1.2a, it names the problem (concentrated ownership of land, money creation, and enterprise) and cites compatible literature without adopting a mechanism. This makes Doughnut Economics' case for C1.2b *weaker* than either Georgism's (which commits to capturing land rent specifically, scoring 0.5 here) or Mutual Credit/LETS's (whose zero-sum, capped design is this corpus's cleanest C1.2b anchor, scoring 1.0) — both of those systems commit to one specific channel; Doughnut Economics commits to none. The 2025 *Nature* update (Fanning & Raworth) itself documents, rather than resolves, this gap at the global scale: the richest 20% of nations (15% of the global population) are responsible for more than 40% of annual ecological overshoot while accounting for only about 2% of the social shortfall, and the poorest 40% of countries (43% of the population) account for only 4% of the overshoot but 63% of the shortfall — a stark, current, well-evidenced finding about the persistence of exactly the concentration this criterion asks whether the system prevents, published by the framework's own authors as a monitoring finding rather than as an indication that DE itself is closing this gap. *(continues in the scoring document)*

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.5. The 'harness the commons' language weighted more heavily

**Centrally Planned Socialism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/cps.md#c1-2b)

Held at the published score; no unit of v2.0 read it. The v1 rationale:

> *Flagged as a genuinely contestable score.* Abolishing private capital markets should, on its face, produce an extremely low measured monetary-wealth Gini — but this system's own Power Distribution finding (C4.4, below) documents that a new elite, the nomenklatura, concentrated real economic power through non-monetary privilege (housing, special stores, foreign travel) outside any formal wealth statistic. A clean Pass would let the absence of legal private property stand in for genuine concentration prevention when de facto concentration re-emerged through a different channel; this score holds both facts at once — extremely compressed *formal* distribution, real, documented *de facto* privilege capture.

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 1.0. The formal wealth Gini, with no private capital markets

### C1.3 Housing Security

Pass Threshold (v2.0): ≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods, housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income. Revision class M (bar, measure or clause restated).

v2.0: 1 at 1.0, 19 at 0.5, 3 at 0.0. v1: 9 at 1.0, 11 at 0.5, 3 at 0.0.

**CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/cco.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. the engine still models no housing tenure, eviction or rent line (Public Trust Housing enters from the member's side only: a 35% cut in the housing component and Acre Equity), so it computes no stability rate; on its nearest measure, a year in which cash income and savings cannot cover the year's cost, 78.0% of adults pass the weakest 5-year window with no such year in the release row and 86.1% at full backing, against 70.1% with no programme; inside Public Trust Housing (20.0% of adults) 79.4% and 87.3%; every figure is under 88%; the tenure forms the owner describes (selling in, buying in, earning in, renting, moving within the system and keeping Acre Equity) are design features the model does not yet carry (reading 3.7)
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. the engine carries no housing-cost line: Public Trust Housing cuts the housing component by 35% for its members (20.0% of adults), and no cost share at 80% of the median is stated or computed (reading 3.7)

Read in group 2.5 (Session 57).

**Nordic Social Democracy: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/nsd.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: cleared. about 90% or more housing stability
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: short. housing cost overburden (housing costs above 40% of disposable income) in 2024: Denmark 14.6% and Sweden 10.6%, among the highest rates in the EU; in Denmark's lowest income quintile, whose incomes fall below 80% of the median, the rate ranges from 44.6% to 60.4% across the EU-SILC series

Read in part (b1) (Session 38).

**Market Socialism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/ms.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. housing cooperatives exist within the cooperative movement, but the market still allocates most housing; no stability rate, documented or designed, is stated, and no source the unit cites gives one
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. no housing cost, and no share of income at 80% of the median, is stated or projected; cooperative housing is a minority tenure

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Participatory Economics: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/pe.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. housing is allocated by consumption councils on needs and effort and described as decommodified and stable; no stability rate, documented or designed, is stated, and no source the unit cites gives one
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. no housing cost, and no share of income at 80% of the median, is stated or projected; allocation by effort rating sets no housing charge that can be read against income

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Integral: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/int.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. community-controlled allocation and cooperative ownership; the rationale cites the criterion's own benchmark, community land trusts' foreclosure rate of 0.46% against 3.26% in 2008-2010, as making 90%+ plausible; a crisis foreclosure rate for trust homeowners is not a 5-year stability rate for the system's population, and no stability rate, documented or designed, is stated, and no source the unit cites gives one
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. no housing cost, and no share of income at 80% of the median, is stated or projected

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Sovereign Wealth Fund Statism: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/swf.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: out of reach. the fund touches neither exemplar's housing market, both ordinary private mortgage-based systems; no housing allocation, land trust or tenancy protection is located
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: out of reach. the mechanism governs no housing cost

Why 0.0 stands: no structural mechanism addresses the criterion: no housing channel of any kind

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Ostrom-Style Commons Governance: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/os.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. community land trusts are the commons' housing form (counted in, decision D31, with a scenario counting them out); no 5-year stability series for commons-governed housing was retrieved
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. nothing in the mechanism targets households at 80% of the median; no cost share is stated

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Universal Basic Income: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/ubi.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. stable income improves housing security for many; no stability rate, documented or designed, is stated, and no source the unit cites gives one
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. the transfer raises income, but without housing policy landlords can capture it through rent increases; no housing cost, and no share of income at 80% of the median, is stated or projected

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Degrowth Economics: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/dg.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. community land trusts, housing cooperatives and rent controls are named as the design's housing mechanism; no stability rate, documented or designed, is stated, and no source the unit cites gives one
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. no housing cost, and no share of income at 80% of the median, is stated or projected; the design names decommodification, not a cost

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Georgism / Land Value Tax (LVT + Citizen's Dividend): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/geo.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. the tax discourages land-banking and raises construction density, but addresses neither eviction nor rental stability; Estonia's land tax did not prevent the Tallinn housing bubble of about 2003 to 2008
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. supply effects are measured in housing-unit growth, not in housing costs against income; no affordability figure for households at 80% of the median is located

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Islamic Finance / Profit-Sharing Banking: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/if.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. diminishing partnership (musharakah mutanaqisah) is a real housing product: the financier bears ownership risk and a markup price cannot grow after default; allocation remains market-based and no stability rate, documented or designed, is stated, and no source the unit cites gives one
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. profit rates are competitive with conventional mortgages, not below them; no affordability floor, and no cost share at 80% of the median is stated

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Mutual Credit / LETS: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/mc.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: out of reach. no housing-specific design element, volatility buffer or stability outcome in any source reviewed (WIR, Sardex, LETS, time banking, Trueque, Grassroots Economics)
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: out of reach. the instrument governs no housing cost

Why 0.0 stands: no structural mechanism addresses the criterion: no housing channel of any kind

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**State Capitalism / Singapore (GLC Developmental Capitalism): 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/sg.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: cleared. a documented stability rate far above the threshold for the resident population
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: short. not maintained for the rental segment, which houses most non-residents: private rents up 29.7% in 2022 after 9.9% in 2021, HDB rents up about 28.5% in 2022, foreigners about 65% of renters; the configured-economy population rule counts them (protocol 3.2)

Read in part (a) (Session 37).

**Status Quo Market Capitalism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/sq.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. no national measure of 5-year involuntary displacement exists; in 2018 landlords filed 3.6 million eviction cases against 2.7 million households, and renters, about a third of households, carry most of the exposure; the published 72%, for market-rate rental only, has no located source (reading 3.5)
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: short. 80% of the 2023 median household income ($80,610) is $64,488, inside the $45,000 to $74,999 band, where 45% of renter households pay 30% or more of pre-tax income on housing and utilities; on disposable income, the clause's basis, the share is larger; half of all renter households, a record 22.6 million, are cost-burdened

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Universal Basic Services: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/ubs.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. zero-rent social housing on a needs basis, 1.5 million new units; Vienna's municipal and cooperative housing, the closest analogue, is durable at population scale; no stability rate, documented or designed, is stated, and no source the unit cites gives one
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. zero rent clears the clause for the households the shelter sector houses, but that design has not been implemented at the scale proposed, Vienna charges modest rents and new cooperative entrants pay an upfront sum of the order of $38,000; no cost share at 80% of the median is stated for the economy

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**MMT + Job Guarantee: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/mmt.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. the job guarantee would substantially improve housing security: an improvement, not a stability rate
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: out of reach. affordability is set by housing policy and land markets; no source located in the job-guarantee literature that pairs a housing programme with the guarantee in its design (D31)

Read in part (a) (Session 37).

**Fully Automated Luxury Communism: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/falc.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. automated construction and universal provision are projected to achieve complete housing security; no stability rate, documented or designed, is stated, and no source the unit cites gives one
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. no housing cost, and no share of income at 80% of the median, is stated or projected; provision is described as universal, not costed

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Stakeholder Capitalism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/sc.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. the host economy's housing (Status Quo's) with some employer housing assistance; no national measure of 5-year displacement exists, as for Status Quo
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: short. housing remains market-allocated; at 80% of the US median, $64,488, 45% of renter households in the $45,000 to $74,999 band pay 30% or more of pre-tax income, and no source shows the reform changing it

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**State Capitalism / China (Party-State-Directed Market Economy): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/cn.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. about 12.6 million households were moved as their dwellings were demolished in 2008-2012; by 2022 more than 85% of homes were sold before completion, and mortgage boycotts spread to over 300 stalled developments; about 300 million migrant workers were historically shut out of city housing support; no 5-year stability rate is reported
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. ownership is broad and lightly leveraged (about 18% of owners with a mortgage in 2022), but no housing cost share for households at 80% of the median is located, for owners or for the migrant renters

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**State Capitalism / Qatar (Gulf Rentier-Distributive Statism): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/qa.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. for citizens housing is largely decommodified (free plots, long-term loans); for non-citizens, the large majority of residents, housing and residence depend on continuous employment with a cooperating employer, who can cancel the visa; no 5-year rate is reported for either
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. low-wage workers are housed by the employer or paid a housing allowance of at least QAR 500 a month; no housing cost share at 80% of the median is located

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Libertarian Minarchism: 0.0** (v1 0.0). [Evidence](evidence/lm.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. pure market allocation with no stabilizing mechanism; no rate is stated
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. no affordability instrument of any kind; housing costs are whatever the market sets

Why 0.0 stands: no structural mechanism addresses the criterion: pure market allocation with no stabilizing mechanism (the 0.0 band's anchor)

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

**Doughnut Economics: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/de.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: not shown. housing is one of the twelve social-foundation dimensions and City Portraits track it; the one located policy output (Portland's 'From Excess to Access') is narrow; no stability rate, documented or designed, is stated, and no source the unit cites gives one
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: not shown. affordability is tracked as an indicator, not set by a mechanism of the framework's own

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.0. Held at 0.5 on the published reading, which credits the diagnostic process and its one policy output; the reading that gave the framework's C1.2a 0.0 (no vehicle of its own) would give 0.0 here, and is flagged

**Centrally Planned Socialism: 1.0** (v1 1.0). [Evidence](evidence/cps.md#c1-3)

1. *≥88% housing stability over 5-year periods*: cleared. no 5-year rate is documented, but the three causes of displacement the measure names were closed to state tenants: no mortgages, rents and fees unchanged since 1928, and eviction 'virtually unheard of in public housing'; the exposed segment, workers' hostels whose places went with the job, held 6% of households in the 1989 census; counting every hostel household as displaced within five years leaves 94% stable; through the collapse, flats passed to their occupants practically free of charge (reading 3.4)
2. *housing costs ≤30% of disposable income at 80% of regional median household disposable income*: cleared. housing fees, communal services included, were about 2.5% of an average worker's or employee's income in 1990, so a household at 80% of the median paid of the order of 3%; after the system ended, the poorest Russian quintile's rent-to-income ratio reached 18.6% in 1995, still under 30%

Read in group 2.3 (Session 54).

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.5. The 1.0 band's anchor example; it holds

### C1.4 Automation Resilience

Pass Threshold (v2.0): Poverty <8% and aggregate demand >85% baseline in each of the 30%, 50% and 70% displacement scenarios. Revision class I (indicator named for an unchanged clause and bar).

v2.0: 0 at 1.0, 18 at 0.5, 5 at 0.0. v1: 4 at 1.0, 14 at 0.5, 5 at 0.0.

**CCO-PTF-PTH-SZH-CIP: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/cco.md#c1-4)

1. *Poverty <8%*: not shown. v2.0 names the design's own model of three scenarios displacing 30%, 50% and 70% of paid hours; the model at v5.6.0 still cannot run them: its automation wave slows wages and, since v5.4, ends jobs, at 2.377 automation job losses per 100 adult-years in the Adverse environment, with 3.587% of adult-years out of work, and its labour response lowers paid hours by 8.3% in the Reference and 11.3% in the Adverse environment, far from a 30% displacement; in the Adverse environment poverty on the Societal Poverty Line is 8.3% (release row) and 9.7% (full backing) at year 20, above 8% in both (reading 3.8)
2. *aggregate demand >85% baseline*: not shown. the model computes no final consumption expenditure and no aggregate demand against a pre-displacement baseline (reading 3.8)

Read in group 2.5 (Session 57).

**Nordic Social Democracy: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/nsd.md#c1-4)

1. *Poverty <8%*: not shown. generous unemployment benefits and retraining are a buffer; the rationale says the configuration cannot sustain 50-70% displacement, without a model; no model or estimate of poverty on the Societal Poverty Line under a 30%, 50% or 70% displacement of paid hours is stated or cited, and none was located
2. *aggregate demand >85% baseline*: not shown. no model or estimate of real final consumption expenditure against a pre-displacement baseline in any of the three scenarios is stated or cited, and none was located

Read in group 2.7 (Session 60).

**Market Socialism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/ms.md#c1-4)

1. *Poverty <8%*: not shown. cooperatives can reduce hours rather than shed members and distribute automation's gains to them, under competitive pressure to automate; no model or estimate of poverty on the Societal Poverty Line under a 30%, 50% or 70% displacement of paid hours is stated or cited, and none was located
2. *aggregate demand >85% baseline*: not shown. no model or estimate of real final consumption expenditure against a pre-displacement baseline in any of the three scenarios is stated or cited, and none was located

Read in group 2.7 (Session 60).

**Participatory Economics: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/pe.md#c1-4)

1. *Poverty <8%*: cleared. while maintaining consumption access
2. *aggregate demand >85% baseline*: not shown. remuneration by effort and sacrifice, with an average income for those who cannot work, is specified, and the rationale says the gains of automation could be distributed through reduced hours; no level of consumption or demand under a 30%, 50% or 70% displacement scenario is stated or cited, and none is located in the model's literature (reading 3.2)

Read in part (b4) (Session 41).

**Integral: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/int.md#c1-4)

1. *Poverty <8%*: not shown. the white paper states that essentials remain accessible 'regardless of age or ability' and that automation lowers the labour needed for them, but access still 'emerges from participation' and redemption needs an ITC balance; it states no level of poverty under any displacement scenario and invites readers to stress-test it; no model or estimate of poverty on the Societal Poverty Line under a 30%, 50% or 70% displacement of paid hours is stated or cited, and none was located
2. *aggregate demand >85% baseline*: not shown. production planned on needs assessed through CDS, not on purchasing power, so demand does not require employment; no model or estimate of real final consumption expenditure against a pre-displacement baseline in any of the three scenarios is stated or cited, and none was located

Read in group 2.7 (Session 60).

**Sovereign Wealth Fund Statism: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/swf.md#c1-4)

1. *Poverty <8%*: not shown. the fund's revenue does not erode with the wage base, but no payout scales with displacement (Norway's budget transfer; Alaska's fixed dividend); no model or estimate of poverty on the Societal Poverty Line under a 30%, 50% or 70% displacement of paid hours is stated or cited, and none was located
2. *aggregate demand >85% baseline*: not shown. no model or estimate of real final consumption expenditure against a pre-displacement baseline in any of the three scenarios is stated or cited, and none was located

Read in group 2.7 (Session 60).

**Ostrom-Style Commons Governance: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/os.md#c1-4)

1. *Poverty <8%*: not shown. a commons share is not conditioned on employment and its non-wage income is observed, for members of a bounded resource; no model or estimate of poverty on the Societal Poverty Line under a 30%, 50% or 70% displacement of paid hours is stated or cited, and none was located
2. *aggregate demand >85% baseline*: not shown. no aggregate-demand mechanism, as the rationale says; no model or estimate of real final consumption expenditure against a pre-displacement baseline in any of the three scenarios is stated or cited, and none was located

Read in group 2.7 (Session 60).

Close call: a reasonable scorer could set 0.0.

**Universal Basic Income: 0.5** (v1 1.0, down). [Evidence](evidence/ubi.md#c1-4)

1. *Poverty <8%*: not shown. an unconditional transfer is an explicit mechanism decoupling income from employment; the rationale's 'scales naturally with automation' is a design statement, not a level of poverty under a stated scenario; no model or estimate of poverty on the Societal Poverty Line under a 30%, 50% or 70% displacement of paid hours is stated or cited, and none was located
2. *aggregate demand >85% baseline*: not shown. unconditional transfers sustain household spending, as the rationale says; no model or estimate of real final consumption expenditure against a pre-displacement baseline in any of the three scenarios is stated or cited, and none was located

Read in group 2.7 (Session 60).

**Degrowth Economics: 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/dg.md#c1-4)

1. *Poverty <8%*: not shown. reduced hours and work-sharing could absorb displacement; no mechanism for income as labour becomes optional is specified; no model or estimate of poverty on the Societal Poverty Line under a 30%, 50% or 70% displacement of paid hours is stated or cited, and none was located
2. *aggregate demand >85% baseline*: not shown. no model or estimate of real final consumption expenditure against a pre-displacement baseline in any of the three scenarios is stated or cited, and none was located

Read in group 2.7 (Session 60).

**Georgism / Land Value Tax (LVT + Citizen's Dividend): 0.5** (v1 0.5). [Evidence](evidence/geo.md#c1-4)

1. *Poverty <8%*: not shown. an LVT-funded dividend would capture automation's surplus to the extent it capitalises into land values, an assumption less secure for AI-era capital; no model or estimate of poverty on the Societal Poverty Line under a 30%, 50% or 70% displacement of paid hours is stated or cited, and none was located
